```text
artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex:10:PM-KVQ~\cite{pmkvq-iclr2026} shows that progressive quantization---gradually reducing bit-width over the generation process---mitigates cumulative error in long-CoT reasoning, implicitly introducing a temporal dimension to compression severity that resonates with our finding that compound interaction is phase-dependent.
artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex:14:These works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.
artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex:19:These methods each optimize a single pruning axis without characterizing compound interactions.
artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex:22:ThinKV~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.
artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex:28:Our work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.
artifacts/_project/paper/conclusion.tex:5:These findings provide actionable insights for phase-aware sparsity allocation in compound inference systems. Future work could explore whether these patterns generalize to other reasoning domains, provide causal validation of importance discrimination collapse, and systematically characterize asymmetric compound interactions.
artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_robustness.tex:144:[Omitted long matching line]
artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_robustness.tex:156:[Omitted long matching line]
artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_robustness.tex:162:At $s{=}0.30$, reasoning-phase PPL inflation ($11.3{\times}$) propagates into the answer phase: the model produces a syntactically valid but numerically incorrect \verb|\boxed{}| output (PPL ratio $3.1{\times}$ on answer tokens vs.\ $1.1{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$). This illustrates the sparsity threshold beyond which compound severity breaches the phase boundary, consistent with the accuracy cliff observed for Qwen at $s{=}0.30$ ($\delta{=}-27$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:accuracy}).
artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_robustness.tex:165:These examples illustrate that pruning-induced compound severity is phase-selective: non-think tokens are relatively protected at moderate sparsity ($s \leq 0.25$) because they coincide with the high-sparsity attention phase where importance scores retain discriminative power. At higher sparsity ($s{=}0.30$), discrimination collapse (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}) erodes this protection, and compound severity spills into the answer phase.

artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_disc_analysis.tex:53:These effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.
artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_disc_analysis.tex:163:This dual-regime structure complements the importance discrimination collapse analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}): at low sparsity, compound interaction primarily manifests as increased verbosity (degraded attention produces repetitive CoT); at high sparsity, the interaction additionally corrupts reasoning-critical computations.
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:7:Prior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:9:Phase-aware methods like ThinKV \cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from score collapse \cite{hies-2025}, greedy bias \cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, and temporal dynamics \cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple axes are composed.
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:10:If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:23:    \item We characterize a \textbf{phase-modulated compound severity gradient}: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonic dose-response ($\rho{=}1.0$; $\Delta$: $0.32 \to 9.94$) with a phase gradient $2.7$--$15{\times}$ larger than random eviction where both strategies are significant ($s \geq 0.25$) on MATH-500; the gradient direction is consistent on GSM8K, while strategy specificity attenuates (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
artifacts/_project/paper/appendix.tex:38:[Omitted long matching line]
artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex:8:These findings reveal that compound degradation concentrates in specific reasoning phases, challenging the assumption of uniform compound interaction.
```

```text
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:17:Cross-benchmark validation on GSM8K reveals consistent phase gradient direction for both models (DeepSeek: $\Delta$ $0.241 \to 4.682$; Qwen: $0.039 \to 2.092$), while strategy specificity (importance/random gradient ratio) attenuates from $15$--$36{\times}$ on MATH-500 to $2.3$--$3.8{\times}$ on GSM8K, indicating that phase modulation generalizes but its magnitude is task-dependent.
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:23:    \item We characterize a \textbf{phase-modulated compound severity gradient}: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonic dose-response ($\rho{=}1.0$; $\Delta$: $0.32 \to 9.94$) with a phase gradient $2.7$--$15{\times}$ larger than random eviction where both strategies are significant ($s \geq 0.25$) on MATH-500; the gradient direction is consistent on GSM8K, while strategy specificity attenuates (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:26:    \item We validate the phase gradient on \textbf{GSM8K} for both models, finding that phase modulation is benchmark-robust while strategy specificity is task-dependent (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented a systematic empirical investigation showing that compound sparsity severity is not phase-invariant but exhibits an emergent phase-strategy interaction. Under importance-based eviction, the paired median difference $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (DeepSeek, $n{=}500$; Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$) and $0.07$ to $3.16$ (Qwen, $n{=}475$), all $p < 10^{-13}$. Random eviction is $15$--$17{\times}$ weaker where both strategies are significant ($s \geq 0.25$). A model-dependent crossover on DeepSeek shows that no single strategy dominates uniformly. Per-phase Gini analysis reveals importance discrimination collapse as a correlational diagnostic: despite near-identical aggregate Gini drops, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning.
5	These findings provide actionable insights for phase-aware sparsity allocation in compound inference systems. Future work could explore whether these patterns generalize to other reasoning domains, provide causal validation of importance discrimination collapse, and systematically characterize asymmetric compound interactions.
6	
```

</details>

```text
1	% Section 3: Background and Problem Setup
2	% Note: eq:ii and eq:gradient are the canonical definitions.
3	% Full formulation details in Appendix (app:formulation-details).
4	
5	\section{Background and Problem Setup}
6	\label{sec:background}
7	
8	We formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}
11	Let $s \in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.
12	
13	\paragraph{Interaction Index.}
14	For a text segment, let $\text{PPL}_c$ denote perplexity under condition $c \in \{d, a, k, b\}$ (dense, attention-only, KV-only, compound). Normalizing by the dense baseline yields $r_c = \text{PPL}_c / \text{PPL}_d$, and:
15	\begin{equation}
16	    \mathrm{II} = r_b - r_a - r_k + 1.
17	    \label{eq:ii}
18	\end{equation}
19	$\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null as primary: it assumes $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (compound loss equals sum of individual losses), the most conservative independence assumption. Although the two axes are mechanistically linked (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null} reports parallel results under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$); the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$), supporting null-choice invariance. The II distribution is heavily right-skewed; we report medians throughout.
20	
21	\paragraph{Severity gradient.}
22	To capture phase variation, the per-problem \emph{amplification ratio} is:
23	\begin{equation}
24	    G^{(i)} = \frac{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{low}}}{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{high}}},
25	    \label{eq:gradient}
26	\end{equation}
27	where $\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G$ serves as an auxiliary descriptive ratio that illustrates the degree of phase concentration; the primary statistical evidence for the severity gradient rests on $\Delta$ and paired Wilcoxon tests (\S\ref{sec:phase-amplification}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}
30	\label{par:phase-segmentation}
31	Inspired by ThinKV's phase-aware perspective on reasoning-time compression~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}, each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, using a quantile-based segmentation rather than explicit reasoning delimiters (e.g., \texttt{&lt;think>} tokens). Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation). We note that this segmentation is an \emph{operationalization choice}: attention sparsity serves as a continuous proxy for reasoning phase rather than a theoretically grounded boundary. We validate robustness to segmentation granularity (tercile/quartile/quintile; Appendix~\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length (Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}). Validation against DeepSeek's explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} markers using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation yields substantial agreement ($\kappa{=}0.88$, AUC-ROC${=}0.98$; $n{=}48$; Appendix~\ref{app:think-validation}). Details in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
32
```

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental-setup}
3	
4	\paragraph{Models.}
5	We evaluate on two 7B-parameter models sharing the Qwen2 backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA): \textbf{DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B}~\cite{deepseek-r1-2025}, distilled from DeepSeek-R1 with explicit chain-of-thought traces yielding pronounced phase structure; and \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct}~\cite{qwen25-2024}, with standard instruction tuning, providing a cross-training-paradigm replication target. Both are loaded in \texttt{bfloat16} with eager attention.\footnote{An earlier \texttt{float16} configuration produced sporadic NaN values; \texttt{bfloat16} eliminated all occurrences.}
6	
7	\paragraph{Dataset.}
8	We evaluate all 500 problems from MATH-500~\cite{math500-neurips2021}. Each problem is decoded greedily (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048). Problems with degenerate phase assignments (null low or high phase) are excluded, yielding effective sample sizes of $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded). Qwen exhibits a higher mid-phase null rate (45.2\% vs.\ 10.2\% for DeepSeek) due to lower temporal autocorrelation in its attention sparsity patterns (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\ $0.894$, $d{=}-1.68$); this does not affect the primary gradient metric $\Delta = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high}}$, which requires only low and high phases. Sensitivity analysis across $\texttt{min\_phase\_length} \in \{5, 10, 20\}$ supports robustness (Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Sparsity configuration.}
11	We apply symmetric compound sparsity at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, computing perplexity under four conditions: dense, attention-only, KV-only, and compound. We compare \emph{importance-based} eviction (position-normalized cumulative attention score, Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance}; BOS always retained) and \emph{random} eviction (uniform). Heads are pruned per layer by Frobenius norm; under random eviction, both heads and positions are selected uniformly.
12	
13	\paragraph{Phase segmentation and metrics.}
14	Each sequence is segmented into three phases using the quantile-based method (\S\ref{par:phase-segmentation}), with $\texttt{min\_phase\_length}{=}10$. We compute segment-level perplexity and II (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}), reporting medians due to heavy right-skew (mean/median ratios up to 43:1). The amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}. Robustness to segmentation granularity (quartile, quintile; Appendix~\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length ($5$, $10$, $20$ tokens; Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}) is verified.
15	
16	\paragraph{Statistical analysis.}
17	\label{par:stats}
18	All reported confidence intervals use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed$=$42)~\cite{efron-1993}. Significance tests use Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired within-model) or Mann-Whitney $U$ (cross-training-paradigm), with Bonferroni correction at family-wise $k{=}4$ (per model$\times$strategy combination, 4 sparsity levels testing one dose-response hypothesis): each family tests a single directional question (``does $\Delta$ increase with $s$?'') under fixed model and strategy, making $k{=}4$ the natural grouping. Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 tests is reported as a robustness check in Appendix~\ref{app:bh-fdr}; all Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR. Dose-response monotonicity is supported by the Jonckheere-Terpstra test ($z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), which uses per-problem values (${\sim}496$ per group) rather than aggregated medians.\footnote{Spearman rank correlation on the four aggregate medians ($\rho{=}1.0$, $n{=}4$; interpret with caution given the small number of levels) is consistent; we rely on the JT test as primary evidence.} Scaling from $n{=}50$ (profiling) to $n{=}500$ narrowed bootstrap CI widths by $2.5$--$5.9{\times}$ across all 16 conditions, showing precision gains. The paired median difference $\Delta$ is the primary metric; $G$ (amplification ratio, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric} as an auxiliary illustrative ratio.\footnote{Experiments on 4$\times$ professional GPUs with 48\,GB memory.}
19	
```

```text
8:Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.
29:Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the per-phase breakdown. Under importance-based eviction, the median II in the low-sparsity phase rises from $0.357$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $8.456$ ($s{=}0.30$), while the high-sparsity phase remains near zero ($0.003$--$0.038$), yielding paired median differences $\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (all $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction shows a detectable but much weaker phase gradient at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$), $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than importance-based at matched sparsity. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes the dose-response relationship for both models. The per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides an auxiliary illustrative perspective and is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}.
35:\caption{Per-phase interaction index (II) at $n{=}496$/$475$. $\Delta{=}\widetilde{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}}$: median of per-problem paired differences (not the difference of column medians) with bootstrap 95\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G$ values reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}. Profiling-subset values ($n{=}50$) in Table~\ref{tab:profiling-subset}.}
39:$s$ & Strategy & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}}$ & $\Delta$\; [95\% CI] & $p_{\text{Bonf}}$ \\
74:\caption{Paired median difference $\Delta$ (primary metric) as a function of sparsity level $s$ for DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$, left) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}475$, right). Importance-based eviction (red) exhibits exponentially increasing $\Delta$ ($0.39$--$8.48$ for DeepSeek, $0.07$--$3.16$ for Qwen); random eviction (blue) remains near zero at low sparsity (ns) and shows weak effects at $s \geq 0.25$. Error bars show asymmetric bootstrap 95\% CIs. DeepSeek importance effects are ${\sim}2.7{\times}$ larger than Qwen, consistent with the training-paradigm contrast (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Significance: {*}{*}{*}\,$p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.001$; {*}{*}\,$p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.01$; ns\,$p_{\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}
88:Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \S\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat: 45.2\% of Qwen problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation ($r{=}0.718$ vs.\ $0.894$); this does not affect $\Delta$ (Appendix~\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}). Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-details}.
101:A power analysis on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) reveals a strategy-dependent asymmetry in downstream accuracy. Under random head removal, all 8 conditions (2 models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels) are non-significant (max $|d|{=}0.164$). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $n{=}50$ can detect effects of $d \geq 0.40$ ($\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$); all random conditions showed $|d| \leq 0.164$, meaning the null results may reflect insufficient power rather than true absence of effect. Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \geq 0.25$ ($d \approx 0.63$, power ${>}\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.
106:Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports the paired median difference $\Delta$ under importance-based eviction for both models at $n{=}496$/$475$. All eight conditions yield CIs excluding zero; within-importance dose-response $d_z$ (s015 vs.\ s030) is $0.94$ (DeepSeek) and $0.92$ (Qwen), both large effects. Importance-based eviction produces significantly larger phase gradients than random at all four sparsity levels ($p_{\text{Bonf}} \ll 10^{-10}$, Wilcoxon; permutation tests in Appendix~\ref{app:permutation-details}).
111:\caption{Median of paired differences ($\Delta_i = \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction with bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}
115:$s$ & DS ($n{=}496$): $\widetilde{\Delta}$\; [95\% CI] & QW ($n{=}475$): $\widetilde{\Delta}$\; [95\% CI] \\
129:Across both models, 93.2\% of answer tokens (\verb|\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal. This non-uniformity identifies \emph{where} severity concentrates: reasoning-trace tokens show $5.7$--$43.5{\times}$ PPL degradation vs.\ $1.3$--$5.3{\times}$ for answer tokens (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). However, reasoning-trace degradation may produce \emph{silent reasoning errors}---correct formatting with corrupted intermediate logic \cite{lanham-2023-faithfulness, turpin-2023-biased-cot}---a failure mode that PPL detects but answer-level accuracy may miss.
134:To validate that the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we evaluate MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192; Table~\ref{tab:accuracy}). Qwen's minimal truncation ($\leq 4\%$) provides a clean PPL-accuracy bridge.
135:Qwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\leq 4\%$) shows gradual decline from $46\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\,pp) with a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\%$, $\delta{=}-27$\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) degrades more steeply ($74\% \to 4\%$) with a dual-regime structure: at $s \leq 0.20$, degradation is primarily truncation-driven (non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\%$ at $s{=}0.15$--$0.20$ vs.\ $88\%$ dense); at $s \geq 0.25$, both truncation and reasoning quality collapse simultaneously (Appendix~\ref{app:accuracy-details}).
136:The two models exhibit distinct failure modes reflecting training-paradigm dependence: DeepSeek failures are \emph{truncation-dominated} (truncation $16\% \to 76\%$), while Qwen failures are \emph{wrong-answer-dominated} (truncation $\leq 4\%$, reasoning quality degrades directly). Given minimal truncation, Qwen accuracy data provide a reliable PPL-accuracy bridge; DeepSeek accuracy results, confounded by truncation rates of $16\%$--$76\%$, should be interpreted as qualitative observations only.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VuDjSjAicuxfoEXBQdsRFv`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Compound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.
3	We find that compound sparsity severity is not a fixed constant but an emergent property of the interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
4	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonically increasing phase--severity gradient: the paired median difference $\Delta$ rises from $0.32$ at sparsity $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).
5	Random eviction develops a detectable gradient only above $s \approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\times}$ smaller magnitude.
6	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; $\rho{=}1.0$), shows consistent phase gradient direction on GSM8K for both models, and exhibits compatible direction on LlamaForCausalLM ($n{=}49$; strategy${\times}$phase interaction weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models).
7	Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this severity divergence.
8	These findings reveal that compound degradation concentrates in specific reasoning phases, challenging the assumption of uniform compound interaction.
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Long-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.
5	Two complementary responses---\emph{attention head pruning} \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \emph{KV cache eviction} \cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---are increasingly deployed simultaneously as \emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations show that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.
6	
7	Prior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.
8	This assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose chain-of-thought generations \cite{wei-neurips2022-cot} alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.
9	Phase-aware methods like ThinKV \cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from score collapse \cite{hies-2025}, greedy bias \cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, and temporal dynamics \cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple axes are composed.
10	If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
11	
12	We find that compound sparsity severity is an emergent property of the three-way interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
13	Under importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity reasoning phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.
14	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), the paired median difference rises from $0.32$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).
15	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; all $p < 0.005$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$), where the phase gradient direction is consistent but strategy${\times}$phase interaction is weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models.
16	Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\times}$ smaller magnitude than importance-based (where both strategies reach significance at $s \geq 0.25$), supporting that the dominant gradient is strategy-specific.
17	Cross-benchmark validation on GSM8K reveals consistent phase gradient direction for both models (DeepSeek: $\Delta$ $0.241 \to 4.682$; Qwen: $0.039 \to 2.092$), while strategy specificity (importance/random gradient ratio) attenuates from $15$--$36{\times}$ on MATH-500 to $2.3$--$3.8{\times}$ on GSM8K, indicating that phase modulation generalizes but its magnitude is task-dependent.
18	
19	Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this phase--strategy divergence. Both strategies exhibit consistent Gini drops across all tested conditions, yet severity diverges by $2.7$--$15{\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.
20	
21	Our contributions:
22	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	    \item We characterize a \textbf{phase-modulated compound severity gradient}: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonic dose-response ($\rho{=}1.0$; $\Delta$: $0.32 \to 9.94$) with a phase gradient $2.7$--$15{\times}$ larger than random eviction where both strategies are significant ($s \geq 0.25$) on MATH-500; the gradient direction is consistent on GSM8K, while strategy specificity attenuates (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
24	    \item We replicate the pattern \textbf{across models and architectures}: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; $\rho{=}1.0$) and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B ($n{=}49$; consistent direction, weaker strategy${\times}$phase contrast; \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
25	    \item We identify \textbf{importance discrimination collapse} via per-phase Gini analysis as a correlational diagnostic: consistent collapse across models and strategies, with the structural specificity of importance-based pruning corresponding to the severity divergence (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
26	    \item We validate the phase gradient on \textbf{GSM8K} for both models, finding that phase modulation is benchmark-robust while strategy specificity is task-dependent (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

```text
5:A uniform compression budget implicitly accepts order-of-magnitude severity penalties during low-sparsity reasoning phases. Pilot evaluation ($n{=}30$, $s{=}0.25$; Appendix~\ref{app:pilot-mitigation}) achieved 18\% oracle recovery (95\% CI: $[-39\%, 45.8\%]$), falling below the pre-registered 30\% gate; the CI spans zero, indicating no statistically distinguishable improvement. Effective phase-aware allocation likely requires finer phase boundary estimation than current proxies provide. Additional mechanisms (e.g., strategy switching, adaptive thresholding) warrant investigation. We hypothesize that phase-modulated severity may extend to alternative attention-mass-based scoring methods (H$_2$O \cite{h2o-neurips2023}, SnapKV \cite{snapkv-neurips2024}, PyramidKV \cite{pyramidkv-colm2025}), which remains an open question for future work. Extended discussion in Appendix~\ref{app:extended-discussion}.
```

```text
8:We formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
11:Let $s \in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.
```

### Tool result

_source line 301 · 2026-05-16T03:59:53.967Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DzPC4TZvDP3eZbGowDfRWm`</summary>

```text
4:The mechanisms discussed here are \emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. Both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops ($<10\%$ difference), yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$---an order-of-magnitude mismatch indicating that aggregate concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning, which systematically removes high-attention-score heads (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}).
7:\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}
9:Head pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops (e.g., DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: $-0.065$ vs.\ $-0.060$), indicating that collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\times}$ larger---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades.
11:This pattern---\emph{importance discrimination collapse}---reflects eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection as score distributions approach uniformity. The effect is phase-dependent: per-phase Gini analysis on Qwen shows that the low-sparsity phase exhibits the largest Gini delta at every sparsity level, with monotonically increasing magnitude ($-0.017$ at $s{=}0.15$, $-0.020$ at $s{=}0.20$, $-0.029$ at $s{=}0.25$, $-0.035$ at $s{=}0.30$). Low-sparsity phases have broadly distributed attention where collapse has maximum impact; high-sparsity phases preserve concentration. \cite{hies-2025} report this collapse under single-axis pruning; we extend it to the compound setting.
13:To provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). The Frobenius norm and Gini share sensitivity to attention magnitude; the large effect size ($r > 0.6$) nonetheless indicates strong structural separation.
15:We note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work.
16:%% TODO: Add per-phase Gini results when available from Agent computation.
19:\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}
21:As a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\Delta\mathcal{H} \in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\Delta\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Importance discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence.
```

_source line 305 · 2026-05-16T03:59:56.909Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019YacGGyrZUSdg1pCQxHg2u`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Compound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.
3	We find that compound sparsity severity is not a fixed constant but an emergent property of the interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
4	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonically increasing phase--severity gradient: the paired median difference $\Delta$ rises from $0.32$ at sparsity $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).
5	Random eviction develops a detectable gradient only above $s \approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\times}$ smaller magnitude.
6	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; $\rho{=}1.0$), shows consistent phase gradient direction on GSM8K for both models, and exhibits compatible direction on LlamaForCausalLM ($n{=}49$; strategy${\times}$phase interaction weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models).
7	Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this severity divergence.
8	These findings reveal that compound degradation concentrates in specific reasoning phases, challenging the assumption of uniform compound interaction.
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex:3:We find that compound sparsity severity is not a fixed constant but an emergent property of the interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/analysis.tex:11:This pattern---\emph{importance discrimination collapse}---reflects eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection as score distributions approach uniformity. The effect is phase-dependent: per-phase Gini analysis on Qwen shows that the low-sparsity phase exhibits the largest Gini delta at every sparsity level, with monotonically increasing magnitude ($-0.017$ at $s{=}0.15$, $-0.020$ at $s{=}0.20$, $-0.029$ at $s{=}0.25$, $-0.035$ at $s{=}0.30$). Low-sparsity phases have broadly distributed attention where collapse has maximum impact; high-sparsity phases preserve concentration. \cite{hies-2025} report this collapse under single-axis pruning; we extend it to the compound setting.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix.tex:38:We verified that replacing the additive null $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) with the multiplicative null $r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$ does not change qualitative conclusions. Under the multiplicative formulation, the interaction index becomes $\mathrm{II}_{\times} = r_b - r_a \cdot r_k$. Notably, $\mathrm{II}_{\times} < 0$ across all phases and sparsity levels: the compound interaction is \emph{sub-multiplicative} everywhere---actual compound degradation is less severe than the multiplicative prediction. $G_{\times}$ therefore quantifies the ratio of sub-multiplicative deviations between phases, not super-multiplicative amplification: a value of $G_{\times}{=}30$ means the low-sparsity phase deviates from the multiplicative null by $30{\times}$ more than the high-sparsity phase. Under importance-based eviction, $G_{\times}{=}21.1$--$36.2{\times}$ (DeepSeek) and $43.9$--$64.0{\times}$ (Qwen) across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, yielding a combined range of $21$--$64{\times}$. Although compound effects do not exceed the multiplicative prediction in any phase, the low-sparsity phase's deviation from the multiplicative null is $21$--$64{\times}$ larger than the high-sparsity phase's, indicating that the phase-gated severity gradient is preserved under the multiplicative null. These $n{=}500$ exact values are more conservative than the preliminary $n{=}50$ calibrated estimates ($\approx$51--183${\times}$), reflecting reduced variance at full sample size. Values are computed using aggregate per-phase PPL ratios; per-problem exact computation is left for future work. Random eviction remains flat ($G_{\times} \approx 1$). The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text as the more conservative baseline: it assumes zero interaction (compound loss equals the sum of individual losses), so rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. The qualitative conclusions---phase-modulated severity, strategy asymmetry, and crossover---are preserved under the multiplicative null.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_disc_analysis.tex:23:We explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases ($n{=}30$ problems at $s{=}0.25$). Oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\% (95\% CI: $[-39\%, 45.8\%]$; Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\% recovery (95\% CI: $[-22.8\%, 66\%]$; $p = 0.024$). \textbf{Critically, 95\% bootstrap CIs for both conditions span zero}, meaning neither recovery estimate is statistically distinguishable from no improvement. \textbf{Gate status: FAIL}---neither oracle nor heuristic recovery meets the pre-registered threshold, and neither is statistically distinguishable from zero improvement. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.

At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests support all conditions with $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d}). /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_moved.tex:5:On DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at $s{=}0.25$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\ $1.113$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$). This crossover is observed at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $4.0{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $3.4{\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $2.6{\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $2.2{\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (decreasing as random eviction severity rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}). Qwen does not exhibit a crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases across all sparsity levels (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\ $1.769$).

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_moved.tex:106:For each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\Delta_i^{(\text{strat})} = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\widetilde{\Delta} = \text{median}_i\!\bigl(\Delta_i^{(\text{imp})} - \Delta_i^{(\text{rand})}\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_moved.tex:166:We computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\Delta_{\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, indicating that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\Delta_{\text{imp}} - \Delta_{\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is supported, but the strategy$\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_moved.tex:175:Head pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, collapsing the score distribution. Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, indicating that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.

Median $\Delta$ values vary by $<5\%$ between mpl settings for DeepSeek importance, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the merging threshold.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/background.tex:11:Let $s \in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:10:If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:12:We find that compound sparsity severity is an emergent property of the three-way interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/limitations.tex:11:Our analysis applies symmetric sparsity levels: the same fraction $s$ is used for both head pruning and KV cache eviction. A preliminary spot check under asymmetric allocation (Appendix~\ref{app:asymmetric-robustness}) supports that the phase gradient is robust to head/KV budget ratios, but systematic characterization of the full asymmetric interaction surface remains future work.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/methodology.tex:18:All reported confidence intervals use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed$=$42)~\cite{efron-1993}. Significance tests use Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired within-model) or Mann-Whitney $U$ (cross-training-paradigm), with Bonferroni correction at family-wise $k{=}4$ (per model$\times$strategy combination, 4 sparsity levels testing one dose-response hypothesis): each family tests a single directional question (``does $\Delta$ increase with $s$?'') under fixed model and strategy, making $k{=}4$ the natural grouping. Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 tests is reported as a robustness check in Appendix~\ref{app:bh-fdr}; all Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR. Dose-response monotonicity is supported by the Jonckheere-Terpstra test ($z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), which uses per-problem values (${\sim}496$ per group) rather than aggregated medians.\footnote{Spearman rank correlation on the four aggregate medians ($\rho{=}1.0$, $n{=}4$; interpret with caution given the small number of levels) is consistent; we rely on the JT test as primary evidence.} Scaling from $n{=}50$ (profiling) to $n{=}500$ narrowed bootstrap CI widths by $2.5$--$5.9{\times}$ across all 16 conditions, showing precision gains. The paired median difference $\Delta$ is the primary metric; $G$ (amplification ratio, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric} as an auxiliary illustrative ratio.\footnote{Experiments on 4$\times$ professional GPUs with 48\,GB memory.}

Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex:82:The phase-strategy crossover is model-dependent. For DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at all four sparsity levels: at $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\ $1.113$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$), with reversal magnitude ranging from $2.2{\times}$ to $4.0{\times}$ across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. Qwen does \emph{not} exhibit this crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\ $1.769$). /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex:98:To assess task generalization, we evaluate both models on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. On DeepSeek (clean data, no output truncation), importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels with a strict dose-response: $\Delta{=}0.24$ at $s{=}0.15$ rising to $4.68$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all $p \leq 0.0003$, Wilcoxon). Strategy specificity on GSM8K manifests as a \emph{qualitative} gap: importance-based eviction produces significant phase gradients while random eviction is non-significant across all conditions (CIs spanning zero). We attribute this to \emph{task complexity}: GSM8K's simpler arithmetic is associated with more uniform attention distributions, yielding a flatter sparsity landscape; within MATH-500, longer traces exhibit higher strategy specificity (Spearman $\rho > 0$, $p < 0.02$), supporting cognitive load as the modulating variable. Qwen GSM8K results are inconclusive due to generation truncation (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$2047): while directional consistency holds at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}1.14$--$4.62$; $p < 10^{-4}$), absolute values and phase assignments are unreliable because all outputs hit the token limit. Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:gsm8k}.

Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \geq 0.25$ ($d \approx 0.63$, power ${>}\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix.tex:38:We verified that replacing the additive null $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) with the multiplicative null $r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$ does not change qualitative conclusions. Under the multiplicative formulation, the interaction index becomes $\mathrm{II}_{\times} = r_b - r_a \cdot r_k$. Notably, $\mathrm{II}_{\times} < 0$ across all phases and sparsity levels: the compound interaction is \emph{sub-multiplicative} everywhere---actual compound degradation is less severe than the multiplicative prediction. $G_{\times}$ therefore quantifies the ratio of sub-multiplicative deviations between phases, not super-multiplicative amplification: a value of $G_{\times}{=}30$ means the low-sparsity phase deviates from the multiplicative null by $30{\times}$ more than the high-sparsity phase. Under importance-based eviction, $G_{\times}{=}21.1$--$36.2{\times}$ (DeepSeek) and $43.9$--$64.0{\times}$ (Qwen) across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, yielding a combined range of $21$--$64{\times}$. Although compound effects do not exceed the multiplicative prediction in any phase, the low-sparsity phase's deviation from the multiplicative null is $21$--$64{\times}$ larger than the high-sparsity phase's, indicating that the phase-gated severity gradient is preserved under the multiplicative null. These $n{=}500$ exact values are more conservative than the preliminary $n{=}50$ calibrated estimates ($\approx$51--183${\times}$), reflecting reduced variance at full sample size. Values are computed using aggregate per-phase PPL ratios; per-problem exact computation is left for future work. Random eviction remains flat ($G_{\times} \approx 1$). The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text as the more conservative baseline: it assumes zero interaction (compound loss equals the sum of individual losses), so rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. The qualitative conclusions---phase-modulated severity, strategy asymmetry, and crossover---are preserved under the multiplicative null.
```

```text
8:We evaluate all 500 problems from MATH-500~\cite{math500-neurips2021}. Each problem is decoded greedily (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048). Problems with degenerate phase assignments (null low or high phase) are excluded, yielding effective sample sizes of $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded). Qwen exhibits a higher mid-phase null rate (45.2\% vs.\ 10.2\% for DeepSeek) due to lower temporal autocorrelation in its attention sparsity patterns (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\ $0.894$, $d{=}-1.68$); this does not affect the primary gradient metric $\Delta = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high}}$, which requires only low and high phases. Sensitivity analysis across $\texttt{min\_phase\_length} \in \{5, 10, 20\}$ supports robustness (Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}).
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Long-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.
5	Two complementary responses---\emph{attention head pruning} \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \emph{KV cache eviction} \cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---are increasingly deployed simultaneously as \emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations show that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.
6	
7	Prior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.
8	This assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose chain-of-thought generations \cite{wei-neurips2022-cot} alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.
9	Phase-aware methods like ThinKV \cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from score collapse \cite{hies-2025}, greedy bias \cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, and temporal dynamics \cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple axes are composed.
10	If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
11	
12	We find that compound sparsity severity is an emergent property of the three-way interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
13	Under importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity reasoning phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.
14	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), the paired median difference rises from $0.32$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).
15	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; all $p < 0.005$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$), where the phase gradient direction is consistent but strategy${\times}$phase interaction is weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models.
16	Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\times}$ smaller magnitude than importance-based (where both strategies reach significance at $s \geq 0.25$), supporting that the dominant gradient is strategy-specific.
17	Cross-benchmark validation on GSM8K reveals consistent phase gradient direction for both models (DeepSeek: $\Delta$ $0.241 \to 4.682$; Qwen: $0.039 \to 2.092$), while strategy specificity (importance/random gradient ratio) attenuates from $15$--$36{\times}$ on MATH-500 to $2.3$--$3.8{\times}$ on GSM8K, indicating that phase modulation generalizes but its magnitude is task-dependent.
18	
19	Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this phase--strategy divergence. Both strategies exhibit consistent Gini drops across all tested conditions, yet severity diverges by $2.7$--$15{\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.
20	
21	Our contributions:
22	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	    \item We characterize a \textbf{phase-modulated compound severity gradient}: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonic dose-response ($\rho{=}1.0$; $\Delta$: $0.32 \to 9.94$) with a phase gradient $2.7$--$15{\times}$ larger than random eviction where both strategies are significant ($s \geq 0.25$) on MATH-500; the gradient direction is consistent on GSM8K, while strategy specificity attenuates (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
24	    \item We replicate the pattern \textbf{across models and architectures}: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; $\rho{=}1.0$) and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B ($n{=}49$; consistent direction, weaker strategy${\times}$phase contrast; \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
25	    \item We identify \textbf{importance discrimination collapse} via per-phase Gini analysis as a correlational diagnostic: consistent collapse across models and strategies, with the structural specificity of importance-based pruning corresponding to the severity divergence (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
26	    \item We validate the phase gradient on \textbf{GSM8K} for both models, finding that phase modulation is benchmark-robust while strategy specificity is task-dependent (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

```text
1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity}
6	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
7	
8	Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.
9	
10	\begin{table}[t]
11	\centering
12	\small
13	\caption{Median of per-problem paired severity differences ($\Delta$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$, MATH-500).}
14	\label{tab:aggregate-ii}
15	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
16	\toprule
17	Strategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\
18	\midrule
19	Importance & 0.39 & 1.21 & 3.99 & 8.48 \\
20	Random     & 0.01 & 0.03 & 0.26 & 0.51 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	
26	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification}
27	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
28	
29	Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the per-phase breakdown. Under importance-based eviction, the median II in the low-sparsity phase rises from $0.357$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $8.456$ ($s{=}0.30$), while the high-sparsity phase remains near zero ($0.003$--$0.038$), yielding paired median differences $\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (all $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction shows a detectable but much weaker phase gradient at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$), $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than importance-based at matched sparsity. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes the dose-response relationship for both models. The per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides an auxiliary illustrative perspective and is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}.
30	Under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$; Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null}), the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$); we report additive-null values throughout as the more conservative estimates.
31	
32	\begin{table*}[t]
33	\centering
34	\small
35	\caption{Per-phase interaction index (II) at $n{=}496$/$475$. $\Delta{=}\widetilde{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}}$: median of per-problem paired differences (not the difference of column medians) with bootstrap 95\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G$ values reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}. Profiling-subset values ($n{=}50$) in Table~\ref{tab:profiling-subset}.}
36	\label{tab:main-results}
37	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
38	\toprule
39	$s$ & Strategy & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}}$ & $\Delta$\; [95\% CI] & $p_{\text{Bonf}}$ \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\textit{DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$)}} \\
42	0.15 & Importance & 0.003 & 0.357 & 0.39\; $[0.35,\; 0.44]$ & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
43	0.15 & Random     & 0.083 & 0.090 & 0.01\; $[-0.02,\; 0.04]$ & ns \\
44	\addlinespace
45	0.20 & Importance & 0.008 & 1.211 & 1.21\; $[1.11,\; 1.34]$ & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
46	0.20 & Random     & 0.270 & 0.357 & 0.03\; $[-0.01,\; 0.08]$ & ns \\
47	\addlinespace
48	0.25 & Importance & 0.021 & 3.971 & 3.99\; $[3.53,\; 4.36]$ & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
49	0.25 & Random     & 1.113 & 1.514 & 0.26\; $[0.13,\; 0.35]$ & $< 0.006$ \\
50	\addlinespace
```

```text
1	\section{Importance Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The mechanisms discussed here are \emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. Both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops ($<10\%$ difference), yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$---an order-of-magnitude mismatch indicating that aggregate concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning, which systematically removes high-attention-score heads (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}).
5	
6	\subsection{Importance Discrimination Collapse}
7	\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}
8	
9	Head pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops (e.g., DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: $-0.065$ vs.\ $-0.060$), indicating that collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\times}$ larger---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades.
10	
11	This pattern---\emph{importance discrimination collapse}---reflects eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection as score distributions approach uniformity. The effect is phase-dependent: per-phase Gini analysis on Qwen shows that the low-sparsity phase exhibits the largest Gini delta at every sparsity level, with monotonically increasing magnitude ($-0.017$ at $s{=}0.15$, $-0.020$ at $s{=}0.20$, $-0.029$ at $s{=}0.25$, $-0.035$ at $s{=}0.30$). Low-sparsity phases have broadly distributed attention where collapse has maximum impact; high-sparsity phases preserve concentration. \cite{hies-2025} report this collapse under single-axis pruning; we extend it to the compound setting.
12	
13	To provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). The Frobenius norm and Gini share sensitivity to attention magnitude; the large effect size ($r > 0.6$) nonetheless indicates strong structural separation.
14	
15	We note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work.
16	%% TODO: Add per-phase Gini results when available from Agent computation.
17	
18	\subsection{Entropy Redistribution: A Model-Dependent Secondary Effect}
19	\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}
20	
21	As a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\Delta\mathcal{H} \in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\Delta\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Importance discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence.
22	
```

```text
8:Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.
29:Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the per-phase breakdown. Under importance-based eviction, the median II in the low-sparsity phase rises from $0.357$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $8.456$ ($s{=}0.30$), while the high-sparsity phase remains near zero ($0.003$--$0.038$), yielding paired median differences $\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (all $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction shows a detectable but much weaker phase gradient at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$), $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than importance-based at matched sparsity. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes the dose-response relationship for both models. The per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides an auxiliary illustrative perspective and is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}.
74:\caption{Paired median difference $\Delta$ (primary metric) as a function of sparsity level $s$ for DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$, left) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}475$, right). Importance-based eviction (red) exhibits exponentially increasing $\Delta$ ($0.39$--$8.48$ for DeepSeek, $0.07$--$3.16$ for Qwen); random eviction (blue) remains near zero at low sparsity (ns) and shows weak effects at $s \geq 0.25$. Error bars show asymmetric bootstrap 95\% CIs. DeepSeek importance effects are ${\sim}2.7{\times}$ larger than Qwen, consistent with the training-paradigm contrast (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Significance: {*}{*}{*}\,$p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.001$; {*}{*}\,$p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.01$; ns\,$p_{\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}
82:The phase-strategy crossover is model-dependent. For DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at all four sparsity levels: at $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\ $1.113$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$), with reversal magnitude ranging from $2.2{\times}$ to $4.0{\times}$ across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. Qwen does \emph{not} exhibit this crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\ $1.769$). This divergence suggests the crossover arises from model-specific attention head specialization patterns rather than a universal compound-sparsity property. Full crossover analysis appears in Appendix~\ref{app:crossover-details}.
98:To assess task generalization, we evaluate both models on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. On DeepSeek (clean data, no output truncation), importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels with a strict dose-response: $\Delta{=}0.24$ at $s{=}0.15$ rising to $4.68$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all $p \leq 0.0003$, Wilcoxon). Strategy specificity on GSM8K manifests as a \emph{qualitative} gap: importance-based eviction produces significant phase gradients while random eviction is non-significant across all conditions (CIs spanning zero). We attribute this to \emph{task complexity}: GSM8K's simpler arithmetic is associated with more uniform attention distributions, yielding a flatter sparsity landscape; within MATH-500, longer traces exhibit higher strategy specificity (Spearman $\rho > 0$, $p < 0.02$), supporting cognitive load as the modulating variable. Qwen GSM8K results are inconclusive due to generation truncation (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$2047): while directional consistency holds at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}1.14$--$4.62$; $p < 10^{-4}$), absolute values and phase assignments are unreliable because all outputs hit the token limit. Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:gsm8k}.
101:A power analysis on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) reveals a strategy-dependent asymmetry in downstream accuracy. Under random head removal, all 8 conditions (2 models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels) are non-significant (max $|d|{=}0.164$). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $n{=}50$ can detect effects of $d \geq 0.40$ ($\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$); all random conditions showed $|d| \leq 0.164$, meaning the null results may reflect insufficient power rather than true absence of effect. Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \geq 0.25$ ($d \approx 0.63$, power ${>}\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.
135:Qwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\leq 4\%$) shows gradual decline from $46\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\,pp) with a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\%$, $\delta{=}-27$\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) degrades more steeply ($74\% \to 4\%$) with a dual-regime structure: at $s \leq 0.20$, degradation is primarily truncation-driven (non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\%$ at $s{=}0.15$--$0.20$ vs.\ $88\%$ dense); at $s \geq 0.25$, both truncation and reasoning quality collapse simultaneously (Appendix~\ref{app:accuracy-details}).
136:The two models exhibit distinct failure modes reflecting training-paradigm dependence: DeepSeek failures are \emph{truncation-dominated} (truncation $16\% \to 76\%$), while Qwen failures are \emph{wrong-answer-dominated} (truncation $\leq 4\%$, reasoning quality degrades directly). Given minimal truncation, Qwen accuracy data provide a reliable PPL-accuracy bridge; DeepSeek accuracy results, confounded by truncation rates of $16\%$--$76\%$, should be interpreted as qualitative observations only.
```
