```text
1	% Section 3: Background and Problem Setup
2	% Note: eq:ii and eq:gradient are the canonical definitions.
3	% Full formulation details in Appendix (app:formulation-details).
4	
5	\section{Background and Problem Setup}
6	\label{sec:background}
7	
8	We formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}
11	Let $s \in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.
12	
13	\paragraph{Interaction Index.}
14	For a text segment, let $\text{PPL}_c$ denote perplexity under condition $c \in \{d, a, k, b\}$ (dense, attention-only, KV-only, compound). Normalizing by the dense baseline yields $r_c = \text{PPL}_c / \text{PPL}_d$, and:
15	\begin{equation}
16	    \mathrm{II} = r_b - r_a - r_k + 1.
17	    \label{eq:ii}
18	\end{equation}
19	$\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null as primary: it assumes $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (compound loss equals sum of individual losses), the most conservative independence assumption. Although the two axes are mechanistically linked (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null} reports parallel results under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$); the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$), supporting null-choice invariance. The II distribution is heavily right-skewed; we report medians throughout.
20	
21	\paragraph{Severity gradient.}
22	To capture phase variation, the per-problem \emph{amplification ratio} is:
23	\begin{equation}
24	    G^{(i)} = \frac{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{low}}}{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{high}}},
25	    \label{eq:gradient}
26	\end{equation}
27	where $\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G$ serves as an auxiliary descriptive ratio that illustrates the degree of phase concentration; the primary statistical evidence for the severity gradient rests on $\Delta$ and paired Wilcoxon tests (\S\ref{sec:phase-amplification}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}
30	\label{par:phase-segmentation}
31	Inspired by ThinKV's phase-aware perspective on reasoning-time compression~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}, each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, using a quantile-based segmentation rather than explicit reasoning delimiters (e.g., \texttt{&lt;think>} tokens). Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation). We note that this segmentation is an \emph{operationalization choice}: attention sparsity serves as a continuous proxy for reasoning phase rather than a theoretically grounded boundary. We validate robustness to segmentation granularity (tercile/quartile/quintile; Appendix~\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length (Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}). Validation against DeepSeek's explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} markers using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation yields substantial agreement ($\kappa{=}0.88$, AUC-ROC${=}0.98$; $n{=}48$; Appendix~\ref{app:think-validation}). Details in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
32	
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/analysis.tex:4:The mechanisms discussed here are \emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. Both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops ($<10\%$ difference), yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$---an order-of-magnitude mismatch indicating that aggregate concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning, which systematically removes high-attention-score heads (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex:5:A uniform compression budget implicitly accepts order-of-magnitude severity penalties during low-sparsity reasoning phases. Pilot evaluation ($n{=}30$, $s{=}0.25$; Appendix~\ref{app:pilot-mitigation}) achieved 18\% oracle recovery (95\% CI: $[-39\%, 45.8\%]$), falling below the pre-registered 30\% gate; the CI spans zero, indicating no statistically distinguishable improvement. Effective phase-aware allocation likely requires finer phase boundary estimation than current proxies provide. Additional mechanisms (e.g., strategy switching, adaptive thresholding) warrant investigation. We hypothesize that phase-modulated severity may extend to alternative attention-mass-based scoring methods (H$_2$O \cite{h2o-neurips2023}, SnapKV \cite{snapkv-neurips2024}, PyramidKV \cite{pyramidkv-colm2025}), which remains an open question for future work. Extended discussion in Appendix~\ref{app:extended-discussion}.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex:8:Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.
```

```text
5:Two complementary responses---\emph{attention head pruning} \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \emph{KV cache eviction} \cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---are increasingly deployed simultaneously as \emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations show that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.
10:If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
13:Under importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity reasoning phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.
19:Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this phase--strategy divergence. Both strategies exhibit consistent Gini drops across all tested conditions, yet severity diverges by $2.7$--$15{\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.
25:    \item We identify \textbf{importance discrimination collapse} via per-phase Gini analysis as a correlational diagnostic: consistent collapse across models and strategies, with the structural specificity of importance-based pruning corresponding to the severity divergence (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/analysis.tex:13:To provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). The Frobenius norm and Gini share sensitivity to attention magnitude; the large effect size ($r > 0.6$) nonetheless indicates strong structural separation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_disc_analysis.tex:71:Post-hoc power analysis indicates that at $n{=}50$, the minimum detectable effect size is $d \geq 0.40$ ($\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$). All 8 random conditions (2 models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels) show $|d| \leq 0.164$, well below the detectability threshold; their non-significance should therefore be interpreted as absence of evidence rather than evidence of absence. By contrast, Qwen importance-based eviction at $s \geq 0.25$ is adequately powered ($d \approx 0.63$, power ${>}\,0.99$), confirming that the observed significance reflects a genuine effect.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_moved.tex:166:We computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\Delta_{\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, indicating that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\Delta_{\text{imp}} - \Delta_{\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is supported, but the strategy$\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_robustness.tex:138:We binarize each token as ``thinking'' (inside \texttt{&lt;think>}) or ``answer'' (outside) and compare against the sparsity-based phase assignment. The adaptive segmentation achieves substantial agreement: $\kappa{=}0.88$ (per-problem mean $0.85$, median $0.88$), with token-level accuracy of $94.2\%$. Per-problem AUC-ROC is $0.98$ (median $0.99$), indicating near-perfect discriminability between thinking and answer phases based on attention sparsity alone. Of problems, $75\%$ achieve $\kappa \geq 0.80$ and $100\%$ achieve $\kappa \geq 0.60$. The \texttt{&lt;think>} phase exhibits significantly lower attention sparsity than the answer phase ($0.656$ vs.\ $0.763$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.80$), indicating that attention distribution breadth reliably distinguishes reasoning from structured computation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex:8:Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.
```

```text
1	\paragraph{Model scale and architecture.}
2	All experiments are conducted at the 7--8B parameter scale. Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested. Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\ref{app:cross-arch}) provides pilot evidence suggesting the qualitative pattern may extend beyond the Qwen2 architecture---monotonically increasing $d_z$ with within-importance phase gradient $\sim$1.0---but with substantially weaker interaction magnitude ($\sim$1/40 of DeepSeek) and mixed statistical evidence (only $s{=}0.30$ significant after Bonferroni correction). The pattern has been tested on two architecture families (Qwen2ForCausalLM, LlamaForCausalLM); comprehensive validation across more diverse architectures (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention) and larger scales is needed for full generalization claims.
3	
4	\paragraph{Task coverage.}
5	Our primary evaluation uses MATH-500; cross-benchmark validation on GSM8K (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}) supports the phase gradient direction under importance-based eviction but reveals that random eviction is non-significant on GSM8K (CIs spanning zero), precluding meaningful ratio computation. On MATH-500, strategy specificity is quantifiable ($15$--$17{\times}$); on GSM8K, it manifests as a qualitative gap (significant vs.\ non-significant). This suggests that the phase-strategy interaction is robust in direction but task-dependent in form---complex reasoning (MATH-500) produces quantitative strategy contrast while simpler arithmetic (GSM8K) produces qualitative separation. Qwen GSM8K outputs were truncated at \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$2047, so Qwen GSM8K absolute values carry this limitation; DeepSeek GSM8K data is untruncated. Our task-complexity explanation for specificity attenuation (lower cognitive load $\to$ more uniform attention $\to$ reduced strategy sensitivity) is supported by within-benchmark correlational evidence but requires validation across a broader range of task complexities; whether different reasoning tasks---code generation, science reasoning, multi-hop QA---produce yet different attenuation profiles remains open. Random eviction on GSM8K exhibits numerical instability (extreme outlier II values), likely a systematic interaction between reduced-precision arithmetic and score-independent eviction.
6	
7	\paragraph{Eviction strategy scope and H$_2$O confound.}
8	Our importance-based eviction uses cumulative attention scores (H$_2$O-style scoring~\cite{h2o-neurips2023}, Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance}), which creates an inherent confound: head pruning alters the attention distribution from which importance scores are computed, and eviction then operates on these degraded scores. The compound interaction patterns we report---including importance discrimination collapse and the phase-strategy crossover---may be partly attributable to this circular dependency rather than to compound sparsity \emph{per se}. Other attention-mass-based methods (SnapKV~\cite{snapkv-neurips2024}, PyramidKV~\cite{pyramidkv-colm2025}) share the same scoring basis and would likely exhibit a similar confound, whereas strategies that do not derive scores from the pruned attention distribution (e.g., learned eviction policies, recency-based heuristics) may exhibit qualitatively different compound interaction dynamics. Disentangling the contribution of score degradation from the intrinsic compound interaction requires testing eviction methods whose scoring is independent of head pruning; this is an important next step.
9	
10	\paragraph{Symmetric sparsity assumption.}
11	Our analysis applies symmetric sparsity levels: the same fraction $s$ is used for both head pruning and KV cache eviction. A preliminary spot check under asymmetric allocation (Appendix~\ref{app:asymmetric-robustness}) supports that the phase gradient is robust to head/KV budget ratios, but systematic characterization of the full asymmetric interaction surface remains future work.
12	
13	\paragraph{Phase segmentation validation.}
14	The \texttt{&lt;think>} token validation of sparsity-based phase segmentation (\S\ref{sec:background}, Appendix~\ref{app:think-validation}) is limited to a single model (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B) on a single benchmark (GSM8K, $n{=}48$). Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not generate explicit reasoning markers, precluding the same validation. Whether the high agreement ($\kappa{=}0.88$) generalizes to other models with explicit CoT delimiters, or to non-mathematical reasoning tasks, remains open.
15	
16	\paragraph{Mechanism.}
17	The importance discrimination collapse mechanism is a correlational marker, not a complete causal explanation: both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$, indicating that Gini collapse alone is insufficient. Causal validation through controlled entropy manipulation is needed. The entropy redistribution component replicates on only 2/4 Qwen conditions, suggesting model-specific factors.
18	
19	\paragraph{Perplexity vs.\ accuracy.}
20	Our primary severity metric is perplexity-based (II), chosen because its per-token granularity enables the phase-level decomposition central to our analysis; accuracy is binary per-problem and cannot be meaningfully decomposed by reasoning phase. Accuracy validation on MATH-500 (\S\ref{sec:accuracy-validation}, Appendix~\ref{app:accuracy-details}) shows gradual decline through $s{=}0.25$ ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\,pp) followed by a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($\delta{=}-27$\,pp) for Qwen ($n{=}100$, truncation $\leq 4\%$). DeepSeek accuracy deltas are confounded by rising truncation ($16\%$ to $76\%$); non-truncated analysis reveals a dual-regime structure but is limited by small surviving samples at high sparsity. Comprehensive accuracy evaluation across benchmarks remains an important next step.
21	
22	\paragraph{Sparsity range.}
23	Our experiments span $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, sufficient to establish monotonicity at the tested levels (JT test $p \ll 10^{-10}$) but insufficient to characterize the functional form of the auxiliary ratio $G(s)$ or identify potential saturation thresholds. More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics. We do not report inference throughput or latency measurements, which are necessary for a complete deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization. Extended limitations in Appendix~\ref{app:limitations}.
24
```
